  Pretty good lesson, I really enjoyed. And I have a question: what is the difference between sure and assure? I hope your answer. Thank you.
    ↳ **@tommytruth7595** (👍0 · 2017-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SbN7-4zcIb0&lc=UgjFWcNJQxnGSHgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-U3__8UtrQ9ZmEW5
      To assure is more like giving a person some confidence, a promise, a guarantee, to tell somebody positively:
      
      "I can assure you that this is the correct road to NY City."
      
      "Are you sure about that?"
      
      "Yes, I am sure."
      
      The person who is assuring the other that it is the correct road is telling the person that the road  is without a doubt the correct road to use.
      
      He is asked if he is positive about that.
      
      And he says that he is.

      +Scott Shawn You have to reached a certain level of understanding to get what he is saying. This is not a lesson for a total beginner and for learners who can't understand at least through context. It isn't really a vocabulary lesson, but rather a meaning lesson. Words in English can have different meanings and for this this lesson is good to get to know the different meanings. But your critics are quite on point, often I see English beginner lessons in, guess what, English, that is not helpful. If someone wants to teach in English in English, it must assume that their students have reached that certain level of understanding.

**@MsEnglishCoach** (👍0 · 2014-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ew39SSJgb9c&lc=Ugi4cDHuj51Py3gCoAEC
  James, I think mentioning grammar texts only may send the wrong message to English learners.  The best coursebooks in my opinion are those which teach and encourage the use of all the skills: Listening, Speaking (sample conversations,   discussion topics), Pronunciation, Reading and Grammar.  Most of the students who come to me for tutoring have had grammar lessons in school but still don't speak with confidence.  I use books like New Interchange and Passages, Top Notch and Summit, American English File etc.  The student books have audio files, videos and other supplementary materials.  With some students, I use books specific to test preparation, as you mentioned or phrasal verbs, idioms, their specific occupation etc.

**@SabLMPh** (👍1 · 2014-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pXEwNme1YNU&lc=Ugh0WMvVcd2Wi3gCoAEC
  In Colombian Spanish there's the same thing with the word brother. And it has many sides. Some religious people tend to call you "hermano", because they assume we're all God's children, so you don't get offended because you expect it.
  When "regular" people use it there are two intresting scenarios. 1) Some people say to you "MI hermano" (MY brother) and it sounds kind of friendly (some people don't like it, though). Ex: "Mi hermano, ¿tiene la hora?" (My brother, have you got the time?). Personally, I don't get offended.
  2) Conversely, when people say just "hermano" without the possessive it sounds rude. In fact, when you get mad at someone, you call him/her "hermano/hermana" to express your hostility. Ex: "¿Cuál es su problema, hermano?" (What's your problem, brother?) - "Hermano, ya le dije todo lo que sé, ¡no insista!" (Brother, I told you all I know, don't insist!)... It's totally agressive. Some people use it with no bad intention, but the agressive usage it has makes it a bad option.
  Anyways, many like you just don't like any "brother" addressing for the same reason "hey, we're not brothers!".
  
  Thanks for the video.

**@mesmith8347** (👍9 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pXEwNme1YNU&lc=UggN2U5bZ46iy3gCoAEC
  Actually, the words sex and gender are not interchangeable, though I'm seeing this mistake more and more often these days.  Sex is male and female, man and women.  It's the biological stuff that is the same in every culture and throughout history. It refers to things related to reproduction: chromosomes, gonads, genitalia, hormones, and skeletal structure.
  
  Gender, on the other hand, is what each society calls masculine and feminine.   It is cultural and consists of the personality traits and stereotypical roles that society has divided in half and given the labels masculine and feminine.  It varies from culture to culture and has not been consistent throughout history.  Its traditional purpose was to emphasize and call attention to one's sex, not to define it in the first place.  After all, a woman who wears combat boots, swears, and drives a truck for a living is every bit of a woman as one who wears high heels, loves makeup, and works as a hairdresser; the two women merely have different personalities.
  
  Male or female is your sex, not your gender.  When forms ask for  your "gender" they are  unwittingly asking if you are "a masculine" or "a feminine".  One's driver's license specifies one's sex, not gender and we refer to same-sex marriage, not same-gender marriage, to cite two examples.
  
  I think it is important to be careful to keep the usage of these two words distinct, because to use them as if they were interchangeable tends to obfuscate the meanings of both words, which adds to confusion rather than to clarify things.

      Hey Felix, I understand where this confusion comes from because in English, we don't really have a specific noun like "girlfriend" or "boyfriend" to describe someone that you are sexually involved with but not in a formal relationship with. Consider the following: 1) If it is not appropriate to explain to someone that you are sexually involved with someone, consider just calling them your friend. Because in %90 of situations, the person you are speaking to will consider it inappropriate and probably rude if you go out of your way to tell them that your're having sex with someone but arent involved with them. Unless youre talking to your friends or close acquaintances. 2) If you ARE talking to someone and want to give them the information, consider using the words "seeing them". Such as, "No, we aren't boyfriend and girlfriend but I've been seeing her lately". Using those words can insinuate that there is another type of relationship going on, but you don't have to directly mention sex

**@glenfoxh** (👍0 · 2021-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pXEwNme1YNU&lc=UgwnSIN4GqmGrXd1CTN4AaABAg
  Your gender is your sex? Not by today's socially created paradox.
  
  I call it a paradox, due to how the word "gender" is currently ambiguously used by people today.
  
  People use the word "gender", when they mean sex, and by that confuse the meaning of just what a gender is to others who hear them use it that way. And it shows how that confusion has spread.
  
  We derive the concept of a gender by what a sex is. But that's not how it is always used by people. And it has created problems.
  
  Some see gender as a personal internal view of one's self. Those who's sex does not fit what gender they feel they are, claim having gender dysphoria, and by that they are trans gender.
  
  Others see gender as a visual and physiological representation of a particular sex. Be it in how someone or something looks to be like, and/or how someone or something behaves.
  
  Such as a drawing or sculpture of a women is not a women. But it can represent a women to those who see it. So even though many who see such may call it a women, it is only a woman by visual gender identifiers to the viewer.
  
  The same is true for behavioral actions. A person can have the behavior and/or vocal sound of a type of sex, even without being of that actual sex. Case in point, in some cartoons. What you see and hear is not always what you get. Bart Simpson for example. You see and hear a boy, but the voice acting is done by a women. So Bart is seen as a boy, by visual gender identifiers to the viewer only. There is no real boy playing as Bart.
  
  To me, a person's sex is their biological sex. We use words as he, she, him, her, male, female, man, women, to state a type of sex. Where as gender is more of a representation of a sex, in what is called masculine and feminine traits. Traits that when exposed to them, can make a person think of a type of sex, even if it is not that type of sex. Such as what a cross dresser may do to those who see them.
  
  Some however is not satisfied with that, and insist words as he, she, him, her, male, female, man, women are gender identifiers, and not sex identifiers. This creates the socially created paradox I speak of. For if what is used to identify a person's claimed inner personality, rather than body type, the use of those words loose it's meaning for what it is suppose to represent to those who are exposed to it when used.
  
  If I was to say, "I seen a man today." What comes to mind? A person with boobs, and a vag? Or a person who is typically broad chested, and has a cock and balls?
  
  Now that we say a man can be born with a vag or a cock and balls, what now makes the distinction from what we derive on just what a man is? If a man or a woman can be born to have natural boobs and a vag, or a cock and balls, how do we gain a concept of what a man and boy or a women and girl is, both physically and socially?
  
  To me, we no longer do. It becomes a paradox, with no real meaning. Sadly, this is today's norm, being forced on people to think like as what is true to think.
  
  What has become an issue do to this, has it's consequences. That being such arguments as to when and were we should see a person's sex or a person's personally perceived gender as more important. That being the topic of sports for one. Is it OK, to let a trans girl or women compete with natural born girls or women? 
  
  Should a trans gender girl or women, be let in an open shower with other girls or women, such as found at some gyms, school gyms, and prisons, where many may end up showering all together in the nude?
  
  Should we throw out the idea of 'sexual preference' when a person state they are looking to be with a women for the sake of having sex, but it no longer should matter if the women in question has a vag or a cock and balls? Some today insist the answer is yes, we should. and people are being pushed socially to think and say yes, more and more as time goes on.
  
  To me, currently, the word "gender" has become a socially created abomination of a word, we should either rethink as a people on how we use it, or abandon the word and not use it at all anymore.
  
  But, what do I know? O.o

**@stevevai6691** (👍1600 · 2016-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pPvyzXaFWJM&lc=Ugh4Otg6LmuVTHgCoAEC
  First thing you need to realize is that English is a language. 
  
  It is not a subject you can learn. 
  
  The reason why you are still not fluent in English is that you are thinking about it as an obstacle. 
  
  It's a language, just like your native one. 
  
  You need to stop studying it, and start using it. 
  
  Use it for something fun, interesting and useful. 
  
  Doing exercises in it is the best way to be an English learner forever. 
  
  Start using it.

**@phenomenal0310** (👍340 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pPvyzXaFWJM&lc=UggEGm-e4jxHVngCoAEC
  do you guys think those three methods are actually help your in improving english? um.. in my opinion, there are two ways i used to getting my english better than better.
  first, "open your mouth". my first english teacher who comes from New Zealand(i admitted she has kiwi accent but it does not matter) the first thing she teach me is that speak out. i know lots of people are afraid of talk to another people in english. but to me? lol i don't give fuck. before i learn english. i had only known three words 'hi, fuck and shit' those three typical words are such a official language in the world. any way, i was spending 5 hours to talk to english people for learning english. this is more useful than text book. so don't be shy, afraid or feeling weird when you speaking english. i know there will be some foolish people laughing at your accent but not at all.
  second, reading book. i was reading a book every night even the comic book. because you need to consider that one day u could study in english university and there are  numbers of assessment you have to finish. so reading book can help you organise the sentences and correct grammar. there is one important thing u you to know speaking is not equal to writing. they are both totally different. speaking is speaking. writing is just writing.
  addition:
  listen to radio such as bbc or local radio for improving your listening. don't be mess up. because there are five english countries for example, american, british, kiwi and so on and they have their own accent. so if you are in the states just listen to their radios.
  be simple in speaking and writing, simple is perfect.

      刘奕君 There is never 'one way' of improving your English as a non-native speaker. I think the problem is that we've grouped everything together under the 'academic subject' umbrella and we're ignoring the fact that language is like a sport: practice  makes perfect, so practicing over and over is more valuable than reading 101 different books- though that will help you A LOT. My advice to anyone would be to just start listening to YouTube videos in native American or British English- that will normalise the process and will make the writing and reading much more effective for you.

    ↳ **@matheuswagner7051** (👍0 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pPvyzXaFWJM&lc=UgihMyH7nkwrgHgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-FtBI71gdQrpqEQ6
      A very good tip (In my opinion the best) that I think a lot of people don't know, is called "listening". It consists in listening to the same audio a lot of times until you master it. Step by step, you start with the simplest ones until you can go a little further. And when you get to this point, there's a website that has subtitles in English for TV Shows, so you can start listening to even harder audios.
      I'm learning English on my own for like 8 months, and i can watch a regular movie with almost no problems.
      Aforementioned website: http://ororo.tv/en
      I suggest "Friends" to start with 'cause it's very easy.
      BTW, I'm Brazilian too.

**@aizhongguo5812** (👍0 · 2014-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RyxtYRkzqcg&lc=UghuUR95USUp_ngCoAEC
  Be careful when choosing a dictionary. I will give you an example:
  
  Merriam-Webster is the worst choice of dictionary or reference point. Much of the information they give is contradictory to what all other dictionaries say as a simple check will show. Merriam-Webster is also a profit making corporation unlike other sources and educational press.
   
  Let's look at the definition of 'Socialism'. We will compare the  Merriam-Webster definition to the definition given in the Collins Dictionary, The Oxford dictionary, The Cambridge dictionary and Google.
   
   
  Google definition of socialism
  a political and economic theory of social organization which advocates that the means of production, distribution, and exchange should be owned or regulated by the community as a whole
   
  Collins dictionary definition
  an economic theory or system in which the means of production, distribution, and exchange are owned by the community collectively
   
  Oxford dictionary definition
  A political and economic theory of social organization which advocates that the means of production, distribution, and exchange should be owned or regulated by the community as a whole
   
  Cambridge dictionary definition
  The set of beliefs that states that all people are equal and should share equally in a country's money, or the political systems based on these beliefs
   
  Merriam-Webster
  a way of organizing a society in which major industries are owned and controlled by the government rather than by individual people and companies
   
   As you can see, the Merriam Webster definition is the total opposite of all other sources. They are a profit making organisation, not an educational press.

      I'm probably not the best teacher and I know this is from a year ago, but I'll try to explain this, that and it. This (used in the context of an object) is something that you have here, it's close - like... "This box is here". That - is used describing something from a distance... "That dog is over there". It, is used as an object or speaking about something that doesn't have a name... "It is an object"
      
      Don't know how helpful this would be, if it is. And what don't (probably didn't now) you know about the verb seek?

    ↳ **@МаксимВалеев-р5н** (👍0 · 2016-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3VtPci-R93E&lc=Ughq-Lr4KIs2u3gCoAEC.7-H0Z7-Gk4g878lbNyJwX7
      +JT it is not so difficult. The soft sign merely makes the letter more soft. Constants sound more like vowels, but not completely like them. The soft sign plays very important role in Russian because the great number of words will have different meanings with or without the soft sign. For example we have a word "мать" - mother and word "мат" - mat (also word "мат" have second meaning - offenseve, swear or "dirty" words). There is thousand of such examples.
    ↳ **@veronikatender9892** (👍0 · 2018-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3VtPci-R93E&lc=Ughq-Lr4KIs2u3gCoAEC.7-H0Z7-Gk4g8Zsw1AKyRrd
      while using "ь" with verbs, the difference is just in showing an infinitive form of verb. 
      For instance, compare: "I wanted it" - "Я хотел это" 
      and "I wanted to buy it" (where "to buy" is an infinitive) - "Я хотел купить это" (where "купить" is an infinitive)
      
      That's pretty easy :)
      And that was example why the grammar makes sense ))
    ↳ **@veronikatender9892** (👍0 · 2018-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3VtPci-R93E&lc=Ughq-Lr4KIs2u3gCoAEC.7-H0Z7-Gk4g8ZswJXJiNE4
      note that you can't say infinitive with a pronoun or noun (the same as in English). So you should use induce verbs.
      For example: он сказал (not "он сказать")
      она сказала (not "она сказать") etc.

      Kevin Walter Josè Colocho Reyes fyi, you don't need to use the reflective 'me'. Just say, "Could you recommend any book for grammar?" It's implied that you are asking for the recommendation for yourself. If you are asking for a friend who is also present, then you would specifically state, "Could you recommend for my friend...".
    ↳ **@cityhunterfan** (👍1 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3VtPci-R93E&lc=Uggq_tx5QIENAXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-E3ep76fEjzdmTNj
      Молдир Дарибай - say instead, "I would recommend that you read Murphy..." or  "I would recommend Murphy..." As I explained to Kevin ... Reyes, when using the word 'recommend' in this context, you don't need the reflective 'you' except as I indicated. Unless you are giving a recommendation to a third person, it's understood that the recommendation is going to the person to whom you are speaking.

      I think your opinion is an example of ‘badly expressed right idea’. I don’t think there is anything wrong with the American English: it’s just a variation of English like the Aussie English, Malaysian English, Indian English... u know wherever the British Empire was able conquer and left the legacy of using English as a primary language. There is also nothing wrong with the American accent, I know a number of people who are American and speak proper English, that is on par with the so-called British standards. Yet, there is a problem: it’s the lack of education, especially linguistic education. Americans don’t learn grammar, certainly don’t pay attention to classic literature (u know the good stuff whether it is English Dickens or American Mark Twain or Indian Salman Rushdie). But the bigger problem is that they way they, Americans, change English ripple across the world because they dominate media. And if you are someone who is accustomed to a certain (proper) way of spelling or using certain words, this can be really irritating to u. In Canada we have been resisting the Americanization of English for centuries: we retain our unique accents, our Canadian way of spelling and writing, and respect the few ties we have left with the British.

  I think it's more clear to say 
  Bring means moving something or someone. The movement is either from where the listener is to where the speaker is, or from the speaker to the listener.
  and 
  Take means movement with something or someone from where the speaker or listener is to a different place.
  and 
  “farther” for physical distance and “further” for metaphorical, or figurative, distance.

      Adrian Larkins
      Hi, Sr. Well, to start I guess that I have to say that It doesn’t matter much to me what language I spoke or not. My mother language is Spanish, but if another country would have colonized my land (for example England) I would probably speak English. So, again, it’s not relevant to me if I spoke English, French, German, Mandarin or whatsoever language. Luckily or not, there was a process of colonization in the world so that’s part of the reason why some people speak a language. I was born in Venezuela, as a consequence I speak Spanish. In addition to that I learned to speak English because I wanted to and I liked it.
       I do not worry if Spanish from Colombia, Argentina or even Spain is much more influential in the Spanish speaking word or not at all. I speak the language and I can communicate with others by using it. That’s what count’s for me. I think that the real problem with you; it’s the big issue that historically has existed between The U.S and the English People. You guys have never liked each other. That’s probably the reason why you kind of envy North American English to be more popular and world wide spread.

  what is the difference: "set up my computer"  and "set my computer up"?
    ↳ **@kokoybartholome2231** (👍2 · 2014-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LKp79EOEC8E&lc=UghcoXL5AgMqVXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-R5Nf7-VQMqeBnkN
      well, let me try to work it out. "set up my computer" means arrange my unorganized computer. the next one is a bit tricky "set my computer up". in grammatical perspective "set" would act as the transitive verb, and i assume the subject is omitted before the verb, namely "I". "My computer" would act as the direct object of the transitive verb and finally "up" would act as preposition. Thereby the sentence "I set my computer up" means i put my computer above. correct me if you seen it wrong or it ain't sound good. i can only guarantee you that my answer is correct if i was James.
    ↳ **@alexojideagu** (👍0 · 2014-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LKp79EOEC8E&lc=UghcoXL5AgMqVXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-R5Nf72waxiQtRgp
      Kokoy Bartholome
      I'm English, sorry you're wrong here, both sentences are correct and mean the same thing.  In British English, the word "Set" is rarely used to mean place or put. Americans use the word "Set" to place something. British people don't. We only say "Set the table" to mean lay the table.

      김태언 He should have said "substitute it with french fries" because that reverses the meaning. But the waiter would know what he meant anyway, because rice was already on the menu. It's much more common (and much easier) just to say "could I have french fries instead of rice?"

      i swear anki is only good for cramming before an exam, it’s utterly useless for knowledge you wanna acquire for the long term. its system is solid as spaced out repetition does work but the weakness is in the “repetition” part. at one point you gather so many flash cards that you simply do not have enough time in a week to revise them all regularly and effectively and i’ve noticed that whatever you learn from flash cards you forget soon after you stop revising no matter how much time you spent drilling it in. not to mention that you get used to recognizing the specific kanji and words in the format of the flash card because your brain remembers it visually aka the user interface of the app also aids a lot in that recognition, also the fact that in the flashcard you see the term on its own or in example sentences you get used to, instead of learning to spot it even when it’s blended with many other characters. but if you only hear the word in conversation or witness it in an article it would be significantly more difficult to recognize it when it’s not in the exact same size and color of the flashcard you’re using, and when it’s surrounded and connected to many other terms. with languages our brains are used to learn words and characters upon witnessing it many times in different forms, situations and contexts, that way we attach actual contextual memories and meanings to it and you do ingrain it in your long term memory effectively.

**@cirnobyl9158** (👍17 · 2025-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DRpg-s8G8pk&lc=UgwFlPpviZdiwS6HQeR4AaABAg
  Personally I find Anki quite helpful and Duolingo quite bad.
  
  I'm fluent in English, French, and Chinese, and learning Japanese (taking N1 this year). So whenever a friend does Duolingo in any of those 4 languages (sample size a dozen), I'm always disappointed in how little progress they make after months and years of Duolingo. Somehow the gamified system says they're doing great, they've got a long streak or whatever, but in terms of actually being able to digest information they've barely gotten anywhere relative to how much time has passed.
  
  Meanwhile I've never touched Duolingo because I witnessed what happened to my friends, and instead used the Anki 2000 kanji deck for Japanese and yeah it was a painful grind, I'm not gonna pretend like it was comfy. Especially those obscure N1 kanjis like 妨げる (to hinder) which you'll rarely encounter. But language learning is hard. The grind has to be done sooner or later. If you're gonna have to grind at some point, why not use Anki, which claims to make the grind as efficient as possible? Idk if it truly is the most efficient but it's definitely one of the more efficient ways. The grind has now paid off and I can translate Japanese games and comic into English.

  anki is one method that i find a bit baffling when people say "it works." unless you configure the setting before using it, you might start using it and the first card you get could be literally a full sentence in japanese. you just started, you didn't know anything yet, either the vocab or the sentence structure, and you're immediately met with a japanese sentence, as if you're already at A2 level
  
  it makes more sense to use anki later on, as a (supplementary) tool to memorize, not to learn. specifically when you're already proficient enough and you need to memorize what you've learned. but for starters, it's a good recipe for confusing you (or worse, discouraging you)

  I'm a big fan of Clozemaster, where you are given a sentence with a word missing and through either multiple choice or manual text input (the app is super customizable, not just in this regard) you are meant to provide the correct missing word. I love it, though it's definitely not for a suuuuper beginner. Having some familiarity with a language to at least not feel so lost is helpful when using Clozemaster, or at the very least familiarity with the alphabet of your target language helps immensely. I'm not sure that Sino-tibetan languages or Japanese would be readily studied from scratch this way, though Hangul's ease of use might make Korean study from scratch possible!

**@Andymegaara** (👍5 · 2025-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DRpg-s8G8pk&lc=UgwLzqzH9CePx0HMM_Z4AaABAg
  Kanji Study is one of my all time favorite apps. If you buy the complementary add-ons it becomes a complete powerhouse. You have different options to order Kanji based on what material you want to follow (like by JLPT level, by school grade level, by Kanji Kentei level, etc.).
  You can practice stroke order, have a very flexible and customizable quiz mode (there are many options to configure it), you have flashcards for learning and reviewing, you can learn about the origins of each kanji and how it evolved over time, you have graded readers with explanations, you can use it as a dictionary, you can also learn the kanas ( Hiragana and Katakana) and radicals there, you can use a SRS automatic form of study, etc.
  And the best of all, you only have to pay once for each thing (one time for the app and one time for each add-on) instead of the subscription model of most apps.
  For me, Kanji Study belongs to the S tier, not the C one. But I respect that this is a list based on your preferences and personal experiences.
    ↳ **@DeanEGardiner** (👍2 · 2025-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DRpg-s8G8pk&lc=UgwLzqzH9CePx0HMM_Z4AaABAg.AHi53GXIil2AIzImZqFIVV
      I agree. Kanji Study is great. Handwriting recognition is really good. This is how Japanese school students learn Kanji, by practicing writing. The spaced repetition add on is really good as well.

**@wpelfeta** (👍1 · 2025-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DRpg-s8G8pk&lc=Ugyhh3OjGKD0qfsdDt54AaABAg
  I think the best approach is to mix apps. I think it's fine to start in an app like Duolingo to get your feet wet. The very beginning of learning a new language is hard because there's nothing you can read. So an app can make it more rewarding and easier to learn. Then once you have a decent vocabulary after maybe 3-6 months, I'd swap to a graded reader instead like LinQ or Satori Reader (For Chinese, I use Du Chinese, which is very good imo). Then pair that with chat GPT for practicing and roleplaying dialog and asking any grammar or vocab questions you have. And for Chinese and Kanji, you can add Skritter, but ignore the definitions and only use it for the writing. So for me, I'm learning Chinese and I started with Duolingo. Then I stopped early on once my vocab was good enough to start reading somewhat comfortably in Du Chinese (actually it was very uncomfortable in the beginning, but I really wanted to start reading so I just made the jump). And I pair that with Skritter for writing and ChatGPT for occasional conversational practice and questions.

**@gojiranimator4305** (👍2 · 2025-09-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DRpg-s8G8pk&lc=UgxJSaAEBFwsGf7_oNx4AaABAg
  Anki is one of the best ones IMO
  
  Not only for learning other idioms, but also for memorizing anything. Being clear that Anki is just for vocabulary, and not for speaking or listening (Despite having audio options, it's not that good)
  
  The fact is that you need to have good decks to make it. I don't make my language decks for myself, it's my teacher who selects the best ones and send them to me. 
  
  And if you ONLY use anki without adding more and more flashcards you'll get stuck.

**@will_968** (👍6 · 2025-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DRpg-s8G8pk&lc=UgwtgYUjQjQef06oQ694AaABAg
  Very good video. I understand that many people start somewhere, especially when you're a beginner. I started with Duolingo about 6 years ago with Japanese, but I think the problem is when you just use an app as main source for learning instead of a tool. When we use apps like Shirabe Jisho or Anki it should be used as a tool, in combination with native content. I personally believe in immersion and when you get the chance to speak is when you output. But of course, that's what works for me. I use Anki to add words and make my own decks for words that I'd use, and subjects I like (e.g. astronomy, biology, ect.) Everyone should learn about things they like since that's when we really absorb the words. Watch  native content that you like and with time it'll make sense. Overall, good video.

      yesss! we shouldn’t fully rely on an app, but use them as tools. learning off native content was a game changer for me!! still struggling with finding opportunities to output though haha 
      
      making decks for different subjects sounds fun~ and astronomy and biology is pretty specialised! hope my chinese can get to that level one day :))

      Grammar lessons are not available in Busuu  for all languages!

      If the B1 modules only gives you some words it won't help much.
      
      The A1+A2 Japanese course is quite long too.
      
      Personally I have mainly been using Busuu for French A1 (with their formal grammar lessons and quizzes) and I'm into A2 (got stuck there for a longer time). But my progress is slow, I tend to forget a lot, I do reviews or restarted from the beginning and I tend to get stuck again when I can't pass a checkpoint (minimum score is 80 or 85%).
      
      For some reason I don't I haven't been using their B1 course for Portuguese.
      I have plans to review A1+A2 modules first because French audio/videos/dialogues were much harder and the difficulty curve suddenly increased that much after lesson ~20-30.
      Portuguese is much shorter on Busuu than French.
      Need to check what dialogues are there or not.
      
      
      The Korean course seems kinda new, created only 2-3 years ago.
      I found additional Korean tips on their website/blog.
      I expect that it includes more grammar instructions than what we're able to find on Duolingo (newest CEFR/Ai course).
      There are ZERO unit tips on Duolingo and guidebooks for sections #4-8 don't exist.

**@hopefulhyena3400** (👍3 · 2025-09-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DRpg-s8G8pk&lc=UgxcTYKaLnU-7lRp2iR4AaABAg
  I actually like the duolingo heart system.  I think that if I make 5 mistakes in such a short lesson I shouldn't be able to brute force it, and I notice that as I get lower on hearts I start thinking each question through a bit more thoroughly. I think it's a good way to match your pace to your learning level.  I don't really see it as a "punishment", and I don't get that mindset.  Literally the only time it bothers me is when I lose one because of a typo but, idk that's not a huge deal.
  
  I definitely like it better than the energy system they've been rolling out, because you only lose hearts on mistakes, but you lose energy even if you get the question right.  So while hearts kind of let you go at the pace you should probably be going at, energy more or less matches the pace of everyone, and it negatively impacts the people who were doing well while not really doing anything to help the people who were doing poorly.

    ↳ **@siginotmylastname3969** (👍0 · 2026-02-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DRpg-s8G8pk&lc=Ugyw3UqtUwoXdZSLedN4AaABAg.ANojAhYlvIfAS9roWwSM-p
      But straight memorisation is just ignoring the structure of languages to say you have to put masses of effort into every single word when it's not that difficult. It may not be completely reliable but I can tell when a word which sounds identical to another is a spin on a verb I already knew and some more specific meaning/context for usage, or use similar kanji to help figure out the meaning of some of them, or to figure out the reading in a word so I can keep on through the sentence until other words help me guess from context what it might mean. Because I started with grammar, read stuff I liked gradually, learned contextual and cultural stuff as well etc. The proof of your method isn't "I can do this this and this" it's "Other methods wouldn't have been as quick as this" or sometimes "Other methods wouldn't let me retain information as long as this." Do you really feel that way?

      Even if that sentence is not something you would parrot back in real life, you learn how the adjectives, nouns, verbs and other words interact with each other in a sentence.
      And the shock factor of the weirdness makes is more easy to remember because memories stick better if they have emotions liked to them. 
      Even though Duo itself is horrible, they found a very good learning method based on memory psychology which if you re-implement in a better way then that's great.

  Regarding the use of Anki, it's not difficult. The best way is to use sentences related to the content you're studying. For example, if you're studying the verb 食べる, put a sentence that uses it in your Anki: 今日はラーメンを食べる. It's not difficult, and you create a context for what you're studying. I usually make about 5 for each vocabulary or grammar point I study and add them to Anki.

  I think Anki is amazing, I would put it in S (MAYBE in A, but not below). The trick is to always learn words in context and to reverse the direction of translation, or least that's what I do, and I noticed a huge improvement. In particular, the combination Youtube+Anki is super. Every time I hear a new word in German I put it on Anki, and the deck is not from German to English, because many times it happens that you can do it from the foreign language to yours but not the opposite, which is in general more difficult. Instead, if you can do native->foreign you can usually do also foreign->native). So given an English input I must figure out the correct solution in German.

    ↳ **@hehehihi-y1z** (👍0 · 2026-05-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DRpg-s8G8pk&lc=UgxAU7Xg0vNHXwpnpQF4AaABAg.ANA3cSs562WAWel3p7p5A7
      @MUMEN_R1D3R the entire point of my comment is that it doesn’t matter how exactly you use anki, but that the app uses a completely opposite approach to teaching terms than our brains use to learn. we learn languages through noticing their patterns in different situations and attaching memories of the situation we were in when we spot the term. we need to make associations to learn in enough depth to be able to then use that term comfortably on our own. anki makes your brain use visual recognition of the sole term without any live context and it also makes you constantly translate in your head instead of relying on intuition and associations which is the primary way we use language. it’s okayish if you use it to get you somewhat familiar to new terms and grammar patterns, but you will never truly get fluent in any language if anki or any other flash card system is what you use most in your learning journey.
